around an installation instead of actually building it, even though I know the deadline is slipping.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: For stretches I feel lit up and almost unstoppable, like every idea is brilliant, but then a small comment from a collaborator can make me snap in a way that feels bigger than the situation.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I’ll suddenly take on three proposals, reorganize the studio, sketch all night, email curators, and start cutting materials for pieces I haven’t even budgeted yet.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: At my most charged up, I truly feel like I’m about to change how everyone understands installation art and that normal limits like funding, time, or experience don’t really apply to me.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: I can sleep two or three hours after working late and wake up feeling bright and ready to keep going, not tired the way I normally would be.\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My thoughts can move so fast that images, titles, grant language, colors, and conversations all stack on top of each other before I can finish one idea.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: can pull me off task and send me into something completely different.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During my most charged-up work periods, when I was sleeping very little and taking on too much, I was also sure the stains and email phrases were sending me instructions and I sometimes heard the woman commenting in the studio.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: Looking back over this whole period, the energized, irritable, overproductive stretches have taken up more of the year than the calmer stretches, even when the intensity rises and falls.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: There was a quieter month when I was sleeping more normally and not feeling unstoppable, but I still kept hearing comments from behind the shelves and reading hidden warnings into ordinary studio materials for several weeks.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have missed paid deadlines, confused collaborators in meetings, and had an exhibition postponed because my notes and proposals stopped making enough sense to use.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The messages in materials and the voice or smells in the studio have been happening for roughly 11 months.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: The energized, irritable stretches with too little sleep and too many projects have been part of my life for about 10 months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S021\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you use alcohol or drugs, and if so, do you think it could be related to these difficulties with your speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear connection with alcohol or drugs. I mean, I don’t think about it that way when the writing or talking gets confusing.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with speech and writing affecting your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve missed paid deadlines because I couldn’t get the notes or proposals into a form that made sense. In meetings, I’ve confused collaborators, and one exhibition was even postponed because what I wrote just wasn’t usable. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it does affect my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you heard voices or saw things that others couldn't, around the same time as these difficulties with speaking and writing?\n\nPatient: Yes. Around the same times, especially when I’m alone in the studio, I’ll sometimes hear a woman quietly commenting, like from behind the shelves. Or I’ll smell hot metal near an unfinished piece. It feels real in the moment, but when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your use of alcohol or drugs could be triggering these episodes of confused speech and writing, or the experiences of hearing voices and smelling things that aren't there?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. It feels real in the moment, but when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your use of alcohol or drugs could be triggering these episodes of confused speech and writing, or the experiences of hearing voices and smelling things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a pattern where drinking or anything like that seems to set it off. I’m not saying it’s impossible, I just haven’t connected the episodes to alcohol or drugs myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with speech and writing, and the experiences like hearing voices, happen in distinct episodes or are they more constant?\n\nPatient: They’re more like distinct episodes, not constant. This has been happening for about a year and two months now, since I moved from community curation into doing independent installation work full time. In between, I can be pretty clear, but then it flares up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the move to independent installation work or any other significant life changes might have triggered these episodes of confused speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. The timing makes me wonder if the change in work had something to do with it, but I can’t really say it triggered it. I haven’t noticed a clear cause.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It’s not constant, but when it happens, it does affect my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you heard voices or saw things that others couldn't, around the same time as these difficulties with speaking and writing?\n\nPatient: Yes. Around the same times, especially when I’m alone in the studio, I’ll sometimes hear a woman quietly commenting, like from behind the shelves. Or I’ll smell hot metal near an unfinished piece. It feels real in the moment, but when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your use of alcohol or drugs could be triggering these episodes of confused speech and writing, or the experiences of hearing voices and smelling things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a pattern where drinking or anything like that seems to set it off. I’m not saying it’s impossible, I just haven’t connected the episodes to alcohol or drugs myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with speech and writing, and the experiences like hearing voices, happen in distinct episodes or are they more constant?\n\nPatient: They’re more like distinct episodes, not constant. This has been happening for about a year and two months now, since I moved from community curation into doing independent installation work full time. In between, I can be pretty clear, but then it flares up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the move to independent installation work or any other significant life changes might have triggered these episodes of confused speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. The timing makes me wonder if the change in work had something to do with it, but I can’t really say it triggered it. I haven’t noticed a clear cause.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently stopped or reduced your use of alcohol or drugs around the times when these episodes of confused speech and writing happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it that closely. I haven’t noticed the episodes happening after stopping or cutting back on anything, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you used alcohol or drugs in the last month?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I haven’t been keeping close track, and nothing stands out to me clearly from the last month.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with speech and writing could be related to the use of alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern where it happens after drinking or using anything.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F10.1, R47.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m having trouble making sense when I talk or write. Like with my artist statements, client notes, even in meetings, I start on one thing—textures, then budgets, then some hidden meaning I think is there—and people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain. It’s been frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble making sense when you talk or write all the time, or does it happen only sometimes?\n\nPatient: It’s not all the time. It happens more in certain situations, like when I’m writing artist statements or client notes, or talking in meetings. I’ll start with one point, like textures, and then jump to budgets or some secret meaning I think is connected, and then people tell me they can’t follow what I’m trying to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you use alcohol or drugs, and if so, do you think it could be related to these difficulties with your speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear connection with alcohol or drugs. I mean, I don’t think about it that way when the writing or talking gets confusing.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with speech and writing affecting your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve missed paid deadlines because I couldn’t get the notes or proposals into a form that made sense. In meetings, I’ve confused collaborators, and one exhibition was even postponed because what I wrote just wasn’t usable. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it does affect my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you heard voices or saw things that others couldn't, around the same time as these difficulties with speaking and writing?\n\nPatient: Yes. Around the same times, especially when I’m alone in the studio, I’ll sometimes hear a woman quietly commenting, like from behind the shelves. Or I’ll smell hot metal near an unfinished piece. It feels real in the moment, but when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your use of alcohol or drugs could be triggering these episodes of confused speech and writing, or the experiences of hearing voices and smelling things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a pattern where drinking or anything like that seems to set it off. I’m not saying it’s impossible, I just haven’t connected the episodes to alcohol or drugs myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with speech and writing, and the experiences like hearing voices, happen in distinct episodes or are they more constant?\n\nPatient: They’re more like distinct episodes, not constant. This has been happening for about a year and two months now, since I moved from community curation into doing independent installation work full time. In between, I can be pretty clear, but then it flares up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the move to independent installation work or any other significant life changes might have triggered these episodes of confused speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. The timing makes me wonder if the change in work had something to do with it, but I can’t really say it triggered it. I haven’t noticed a clear cause.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently stopped or reduced your use of alcohol or drugs around the times when these episodes of confused speech and writing happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it that closely. I haven’t noticed the episodes happening after stopping or cutting back on anything, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you used alcohol or drugs in the last month?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I haven’t been keeping close track, and nothing stands out to me clearly from the last month.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with speech and writing could be related to the use of alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern where it happens after drinking or using anything. It could be related, I guess, but nothing about it stands out to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you used any substances, such as alcohol or drugs, in the past week?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I used anything this past week, but I’m not positive—I haven’t really been keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Have you used any alcohol or drugs today?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I haven’t had anything today, as far as I remember.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m having trouble making sense when I talk or write. Like with my artist statements, client notes, even in meetings, I start on one thing—textures, then budgets, then some hidden meaning I think is there—and people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain. It’s been frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble making sense when you talk or write all the time, or does it happen only sometimes?\n\nPatient: It’s not all the time. It happens more in certain situations, like when I’m writing artist statements or client notes, or talking in meetings. I’ll start with one point, like textures, and then jump to budgets or some secret meaning I think is connected, and then people tell me they can’t follow what I’m trying to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you use alcohol or drugs, and if so, do you think it could be related to these difficulties with your speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear connection with alcohol or drugs. I mean, I don’t think about it that way when the writing or talking gets confusing.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with speech and writing affecting your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve missed paid deadlines because I couldn’t get the notes or proposals into a form that made sense. In meetings, I’ve confused collaborators, and one exhibition was even postponed because what I wrote just wasn’t usable. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it does affect my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you heard voices or saw things that others couldn't, around the same time as these difficulties with speaking and writing?\n\nPatient: Yes. Around the same times, especially when I’m alone in the studio, I’ll sometimes hear a woman quietly commenting, like from behind the shelves. Or I’ll smell hot metal near an unfinished piece. It feels real in the moment, but when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your use of alcohol or drugs could be triggering these episodes of confused speech and writing, or the experiences of hearing voices and smelling things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a pattern where drinking or anything like that seems to set it off. I’m not saying it’s impossible, I just haven’t connected the episodes to alcohol or drugs myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with speech and writing, and the experiences like hearing voices, happen in distinct episodes or are they more constant?\n\nPatient: They’re more like distinct episodes, not constant. This has been happening for about a year and two months now, since I moved from community curation into doing independent installation work full time. In between, I can be pretty clear, but then it flares up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the move to independent installation work or any other significant life changes might have triggered these episodes of confused speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. The timing makes me wonder if the change in work had something to do with it, but I can’t really say it triggered it. I haven’t noticed a clear cause.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently stopped or reduced your use of alcohol or drugs around the times when these episodes of confused speech and writing happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it that closely. I haven’t noticed the episodes happening after stopping or cutting back on anything, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you used alcohol or drugs in the last month?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I haven’t been keeping close track, and nothing stands out to me clearly from the last month.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with speech and writing could be related to the use of alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t had anything today, as far as I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any withdrawal symptoms when you haven't used alcohol or drugs for a while?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything like that. I’ll start with one point, like textures, and then jump to budgets or some secret meaning I think is connected, and then people tell me they can’t follow what I’m trying to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you use alcohol or drugs, and if so, do you think it could be related to these difficulties with your speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear connection with alcohol or drugs. I mean, I don’t think about it that way when the writing or talking gets confusing.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with speech and writing affecting your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve missed paid deadlines because I couldn’t get the notes or proposals into a form that made sense. In meetings, I’ve confused collaborators, and one exhibition was even postponed because what I wrote just wasn’t usable. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it does affect my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you heard voices or saw things that others couldn't, around the same time as these difficulties with speaking and writing?\n\nPatient: Yes. Around the same times, especially when I’m alone in the studio, I’ll sometimes hear a woman quietly commenting, like from behind the shelves. Or I’ll smell hot metal near an unfinished piece. It feels real in the moment, but when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your use of alcohol or drugs could be triggering these episodes of confused speech and writing, or the experiences of hearing voices and smelling things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a pattern where drinking or anything like that seems to set it off. I’m not saying it’s impossible, I just haven’t connected the episodes to alcohol or drugs myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with speech and writing, and the experiences like hearing voices, happen in distinct episodes or are they more constant?\n\nPatient: They’re more like distinct episodes, not constant. This has been happening for about a year and two months now, since I moved from community curation into doing independent installation work full time. In between, I can be pretty clear, but then it flares up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the move to independent installation work or any other significant life changes might have triggered these episodes of confused speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. The timing makes me wonder if the change in work had something to do with it, but I can’t really say it triggered it. I haven’t noticed a clear cause.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently stopped or reduced your use of alcohol or drugs around the times when these episodes of confused speech and writing happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it that closely. I haven’t noticed the episodes happening after stopping or cutting back on anything, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you used alcohol or drugs in the last month?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I haven’t been keeping close track, and nothing stands out to me clearly from the last month.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with speech and writing could be related to the use of alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern where it happens after drinking or using anything. It could be related, I guess, but nothing about it stands out to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you used any substances, such as alcohol or drugs, in the past week?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I used anything this past week, but I’m not positive—I haven’t really been keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Have you used any alcohol or drugs today?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I haven’t had anything today, as far as I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any withdrawal symptoms when you haven't used alcohol or drugs for a while?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything like that. I’m not sure what would count as withdrawal, but nothing obvious comes to mind when I haven’t used anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms started after you began using alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really made a connection like that, and I can’t say they started because of alcohol or drugs.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m having trouble making sense when I talk or write. Like with my artist statements, client notes, even in meetings, I start on one thing—textures, then budgets, then some hidden meaning I think is there—and people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain. It’s been frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble making sense when you talk or write all the time, or does it happen only sometimes?\n\nPatient: It’s not all the time. It happens more in certain situations, like when I’m writing artist statements or client notes, or talking in meetings. I’ll start with one point, like textures, and then jump to budgets or some secret meaning I think is connected, and then people tell me they can’t follow what I’m trying to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you use alcohol or drugs, and if so, do you think it could be related to these difficulties with your speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear connection with alcohol or drugs. I mean, I don’t think about it that way when the writing or talking gets confusing.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with speech and writing affecting your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve missed paid deadlines because I couldn’t get the notes or proposals into a form that made sense. In meetings, I’ve confused collaborators, and one exhibition was even postponed because what I wrote just wasn’t usable. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it does affect my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you heard voices or saw things that others couldn't, around the same time as these difficulties with speaking and writing?\n\nPatient: Yes. Around the same times, especially when I’m alone in the studio, I’ll sometimes hear a woman quietly commenting, like from behind the shelves. Or I’ll smell hot metal near an unfinished piece. It feels real in the moment, but when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your use of alcohol or drugs could be triggering these episodes of confused speech and writing, or the experiences of hearing voices and smelling things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a pattern where drinking or anything like that seems to set it off. I’m not saying it’s impossible, I just haven’t connected the episodes to alcohol or drugs myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with speech and writing, and the experiences like hearing voices, happen in distinct episodes or are they more constant?\n\nPatient: They’re more like distinct episodes, not constant. This has been happening for about a year and two months now, since I moved from community curation into doing independent installation work full time. In between, I can be pretty clear, but then it flares up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the move to independent installation work or any other significant life changes might have triggered these episodes of confused speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. The timing makes me wonder if the change in work had something to do with it, but I can’t really say it triggered it. I haven’t noticed a clear cause.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently stopped or reduced your use of alcohol or drugs around the times when these episodes of confused speech and writing happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it that closely. I haven’t noticed the episodes happening after stopping or cutting back on anything, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you used alcohol or drugs in the last month?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I haven’t been keeping close track, and nothing stands out to me clearly from the last month.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with speech and writing could be related to the use of alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern where it happens after drinking or using anything. It could be related, I guess, but nothing about it stands out to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you used any substances, such as alcohol or drugs, in the past week?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I used anything this past week, but I’m not positive—I haven’t really been keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Have you used any alcohol or drugs today?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I haven’t had anything today, as far as I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any withdrawal symptoms when you haven't used alcohol or drugs for a while?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything like that. I’m not sure what would count as withdrawal, but nothing obvious comes to mind when I haven’t used anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms started after you began using alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really made a connection like that, and I can’t say they started because of alcohol or drugs.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that the voices you hear are real or that the smells you experience are actually there, even when others couldn't smell them?\n\nPatient: Yes. When I’m alone in my studio, I’ll sometimes hear a woman quietly commenting, like she’s behind the shelves, and in the moment it feels real. Or I’ll smell something like hot metal near an unfinished piece, and I’ll think it’s actually there. Then I check, and there’s nothing there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It happens more in certain situations, like when I’m writing artist statements or client notes, or talking in meetings. I’ll start with one point, like textures, and then jump to budgets or some secret meaning I think is connected, and then people tell me they can’t follow what I’m trying to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you use alcohol or drugs, and if so, do you think it could be related to these difficulties with your speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear connection with alcohol or drugs. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m having trouble making sense when I talk or write. Like with my artist statements, client notes, even in meetings, I start on one thing—textures, then budgets, then some hidden meaning I think is there—and people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain. It’s been frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble making sense when you talk or write all the time, or does it happen only sometimes?\n\nPatient: It’s not all the time. It happens more in certain situations, like when I’m writing artist statements or client notes, or talking in meetings. I’ll start with one point, like textures, and then jump to budgets or some secret meaning I think is connected, and then people tell me they can’t follow what I’m trying to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you use alcohol or drugs, and if so, do you think it could be related to these difficulties with your speech and writing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear connection with alcohol or drugs. I mean, I don’t think about it that way when the writing or talking gets confusing.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with speech and writing affecting your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve missed paid deadlines because I couldn’t get the notes or proposals into a form that made sense. In meetings, I’ve confused collaborators, and one exhibition was even postponed because what I wrote just wasn’t usable. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it does affect my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you heard voices or saw things that others couldn't, around the same time as these difficulties with speaking and writing?\n\nPatient: Yes. Around the same times, especially when I’m alone in the studio, I’ll sometimes hear a woman quietly commenting, like from behind the shelves. Or I’ll smell hot metal near an unfinished piece. It feels real in the moment, but when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your use of alcohol or drugs could be triggering these episodes of confused speech and writing, or the experiences of hearing voices and smelling things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a pattern where drinking or anything like that seems to set it off. I’m not saying it’s impossible, I just haven’t connected the episodes to alcohol or drugs myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with speech and writing, and the experiences like hearing voices, happen in distinct episodes or are they more constant?\n\nPatient: They’re more like distinct episodes, not constant. This has been happening for about a year and two months now, since I moved from community curation into doing independent installation work full time. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it that closely. I haven’t been keeping close track, and nothing stands out to me clearly from the last month.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with speech and writing could be related to the use of alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern where it happens after drinking or using anything. I don’t think I used anything this past week, but I’m not positive—I haven’t really been keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Have you used any alcohol or drugs today?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I haven’t had anything today, as far as I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any withdrawal symptoms when you haven't used alcohol or drugs for a while?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything like that. I’m not sure what would count as withdrawal, but nothing obvious comes to mind when I haven’t used anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms started after you began using alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure.
